Since 2026-10-21 (owner decision) a user's browser windows work in parallel, each in a session of its own, and only their sandbox runs share the queue: `submit` refuses a user's entry past `AGENT_USER_MAX_RUNS` (3, waiting and running together across sessions) with `QueueUserLimit`, before it looks at the queue's length, and the turn fails the same way with `run_limit`, so the page can say that the user's own runs in other windows fill it. The run registry, which answers `409 run_active`, covers every turn in flight and theme toggles per session, never per user, and its stale sweep frees an entry whose stream vanished after the maximum wait (while its sandbox run waits) or the deadline, plus a margin of 30 s. A turn counts as use of the session's dataset, so the idle sweep does not take it while the run waits. `GET /v1/status` reports `waiting` and `in_flight`. Runs that `adk web` starts bypass the queue, because they never pass through `/v1`; their renders still go through the one render slot.

The queue length is the owner's formula and assumes that a run ends within the 60 s rate window. A run that takes longer holds the only slot past the window, so every later start slips by the difference: with one run in flight, ten accepted entries and 90 s runs, the last four wait the full maximum and end with `capacity`. Admitting by an estimated start time (the position times the larger of 60 s per start and the recent run time) would keep that promise; it is an open decision.

### Two steps: data first, then the plot

Owner decision of 2026-10-11: "Use with my data" is an explicit two-step flow. Step 1 is the data, and it ends with the user's confirmation that the table and its bindings fit the plot. Step 2 is the plot, as described above. The owner's principle: in any real analysis the data must be right first, or everything after it is wrong.

Principles:

1. Step 1 is a stop only when it has to be. A clean paste (the parser happy, the bindings complete, no ambiguity warning) shows the preview and the bindings with `.confirm()`, one click from the plot. Nobody with a clean CSV has to talk to an agent.
2. The model never writes data rows. It will choose operations from a fixed set, the server applies them with pandas to the stored canonical CSV, re-parses the result and stores it as the next dataset version with a change list and `.undo()`. The transcriber for free text is the one exception, and its table is confirmed by the user before it is stored.
3. One truth on the screen: the preview table is always the current version. Every model step shows what changed and needs the user's confirmation. Step 2 stays locked until the data is confirmed, on the server: the pipeline answers `not_ready` with `data_unconfirmed` (`load_inputs` in `agents/anyplot/pipeline.py`), so neither the model nor a stale page can start a plot on data the user has not looked at.
4. The most common real preparation is aggregation (single bookings into sums per month or category), so it belongs to the first set of operations.

Built since 2026-10-11 (the gate): `POST /v1/sessions/{sid}/dataset/confirm` stores a fingerprint of the dataset id and the bindings as the user saw them (`anyplot.confirmed`, `confirmation_fingerprint`); the pipeline runs only while the fingerprint of the current state equals it. A new dataset gets a new id, so a paste or a transcription's `.use()` drops the confirmation by itself, and so does any binding change through the `PUT` route or the root's `set_bindings` tool, also one that lands between the confirmation's read and its write, because the stored fingerprint then names bindings the session no longer holds; a library switch keeps the data, the bindings and the confirmation. Since 2026-10-15 (live test round 2) there is one exception for the root's `set_bindings`: bindings that only leave out members of a confirmed number family (`y2`, `y3`, …), every other binding exactly as confirmed, keep the confirmation (`session_state.narrows` and `narrowed_confirmation`, `AGENT_NARROWED_BINDINGS=keep`; `reset` restores the old rule; owner decision of 2026-10-15: "e.g. drops the 2nd line"), so a step-2 note such as "only co2_per_capita, title …" plots in the same turn, and the tool result says `data_confirmed`. Leaving out a grouping, text or boolean role such as `series` (which merged two countries into one line in the review) or any single role, or renumbering a member so that `y1` plots another column, asks for `.confirm()` again. A first plot's `change_request` that stops on `data_unconfirmed` or `incomplete_bindings`, or gets no queue slot, is kept in `anyplot.pending_request`, its `expected_texts` with it in `anyplot.pending_expected_texts`, and the bindings of that moment in `anyplot.pending_bindings` (`pipeline.keep_pending_request`, `hold_request`). A bare `.create_plot()` checks the render for the kept texts; a run that brings a request or texts of its own checks only its own, since the root never sees the kept ones and a withdrawn title must not count as a miss (`chosen_texts`); stored texts are read through the `PipelineArgs` validator (`kept_texts`). A binding write that keeps every binding of that moment and only fills roles open then (the step `incomplete_bindings` waits for) keeps the note (`session_state.fills_open_roles`); a change of an existing version neither takes nor clears it: the next first-plot run joins it to its own request (`merged_request`, at most 600 characters, the older note cut with `…` rather than dropped), and it stays until a run ships a plot (`settle_pending_request`), so a failed run loses nothing. Any binding write (the root's or the panel's), any new data version (a paste, `prepare_data`, `generate_data`, `run_data_code`, `.to_long()`, undo) and a library switch drop it, since it was written for the data and bindings before. The session block tells the root whether the data is confirmed, and the root answers `data_unconfirmed` by asking the user to confirm in the data panel, which it cannot do itself. The page shows a step header `1 data · 2 plot`, `.confirm()` after the bindings, and `.create_plot()`, which starts a run only after the confirmation (before it, the button explains the step instead of calling the server); the eval harness and the development fixture confirm on the user's behalf, because their data is the developer's own.

Built since 2026-10-11 (the operations): the root tool `prepare_data(operations)` with at most five operations per call, each a Pydantic model of the fixed set in `agents/anyplot/data/prepare.py`: `rename` (column, name), `drop_columns` (1 to 10 columns), `drop_rows` (`where` conditions on one column each, all of which must hold: `eq`, `ne`, `contains`, `is_missing`, `gt`, `lt`, at most three; a value is compared, never evaluated), `set_type` (number, datetime or text, with the date format and the decimal separator; to number is for the columns the profile still shows as text, because a column whose every cell carries the same unit is already numbers after the paste, the unit in its name such as `Anteil (%)`: one unit such as `%` or `EUR` is removed and the cells are read as the parser reads a number, the other mark a thousands separator between three-digit groups only, and when more cells read with the other decimal mark the whole column is read that way and the change line says so; a cell that cannot be read becomes missing, more than 10 % of them is a `type_mismatch`; to text the column stays text through the re-parse of every later version, stored with the version as `text_columns`; `MM/DD/YYYY` on a column that is already dates swaps day and month only for a slash column the parser flagged as ambiguous), `aggregate` (by one or two columns, a value column, `sum`, `mean`, `count`, `min` or `max`, and a `month`, `quarter` or `year` period for a datetime key, written as text labels such as `2024-03`; since live test round 5 of 2026-10-15 also `hour`, `day` or `week`, which keep the key a datetime, the first moment of its bucket: a date for a day and for an ISO week (its Monday), a time with its offset for an hour, so "a daily mean per sensor" keeps a time axis; the bucket follows the column's own clock as stored, its single UTC offset or UTC for a column the parser converted from several offsets, and the change line names that offset), `sort`, `pivot_longer` (2 to 20 columns into a name/value pair; a long format over the 2 MB cap of computed data, `MAX_COMPUTED_BYTES`, is an `invalid_operation`; 200 KB until 2026-10-14) and `pivot_wider` (the distinct values of a column become at most 50 columns; duplicate pairs are refused, never aggregated silently), and, since 2026-10-12, `classify` (a new text column whose cells are labels: each row gets the label of the first of 1 to 10 rules whose `drop_rows`-style conditions all hold, else `otherwise` or a missing cell; labels are at most 40 characters, never a token the parser reads as a missing cell such as `NA` or `-`, exempt from ToolSafety's link rule like a rename's name, and the column stays text through every re-parse). `classify` came from the owner's case of 2026-10-12: a generated sine table on `bar-feature-importance` had no text column for the `feature` role, and "positive is class 1, negative class 2" had no operation, so the root had to refuse. Since 2026-10-14, `derive` adds a number column from number columns by one formula of a closed set: `ratio` (`a / b`) and `difference` (`a - b`) of two columns, `sum` of 2 to 10 columns in the same row, `share` of one column in the total of its group (`within` 0 to 2 key columns), and three formulas that walk each group in the order of `order`, a number or datetime column: `percent_change` from the previous row, `cumsum` (the running total) and `index` (the group's first row = 100; the last two by owner decision of 2026-10-14). A text order column is a `type_mismatch` with a hint to `set_type` it, because it would sort alphabetically (`April` before `Januar`, `10` before `9`); repeated order values within a group are refused (aggregate first), and a row without an order value keeps an empty cell; `percent` multiplies a ratio or a share by 100, and a division by zero or a missing input leaves the cell empty, which the change line counts. It came from the owner's live test of 2026-10-14: "which year has the largest share of foreigners?" on a long table (year, origin, count), where the root could only ask the user to compute the column; now it derives the share, drops the other rows, sorts, and answers from the new profile. Since 2026-10-15 no operation loses precision without its change line saying so (owner rule): `set_type number` reads whole numbers exactly (never through a float64, which rounded `9007199254740993`) and counts the cells it can store only rounded; a `sum` aggregate, a row `sum` or `difference` and a `cumsum` of whole-number columns add in Python integers (pandas' `Int64` sum had wrapped around past 2^63 without a word), and a result past the int64 range is a float64 whose rounded values the change line counts, a count the line's length cap never cuts; `pivot_longer` of whole numbers beside decimals counts the whole numbers past 2^53 that the shared float64 column rounds; a date-time keeps its fraction of a second and its UTC offset in `data.csv`; `set_type datetime` without a format reads a year-first cell (`2024-06-01T13:00:00Z`) year, month, day, chosen per cell (pandas with `dayfirst` had read it as 6 January), and reads a column with different UTC offsets (summer and winter time) in UTC with a change line that names the offsets, as the parser writes such a column (owner decision of 2026-10-15); times with and without an offset in one column are a `type_mismatch`, since no time gets a zone by guess. A condition (`drop_rows`, `classify`) on a column with an offset compares moments, its value converted to the column's offset; a value without an offset there, or with one on a column without, is refused with a message that says which to give (it had crashed the tool with a `TypeError`). A condition on a whole-number column compares exact integers (`9007199254740992` and `9007199254740993` are two ids, though one float64), a value with a fraction equal to no cell. A float result (a ratio, a share, a mean) counts its whole-number inputs past 2^53 in the change line, and an integer result that leaves int64 counts every value past 2^53 it writes as a float, also one a float holds exactly (2^63). Since 2026-10-15, `make_date` adds a date column built from whole-number part columns of the same row: a year (four digits), then a month (1 to 12) and optionally a day, or a quarter (1 to 4), or an ISO week (1 to 53), a part left out being its first (a year and a month give the first day of each month); a day without a month, a quarter or a week beside another part, a value out of its range and a row that is no calendar date (31 April) are refused, never moved; a text month column may hold month names and abbreviations in the eleven refusal languages (en, de, fr, es, it, pt, nl, pl, zh, vi, ru), compared without case and diacritics, an unknown name refusing it (owner decision of 2026-10-15); ISO week 1 can start in late December, which the change line says. `set_type` to datetime gained the formats `DD/MM/YYYY`, `YYYYMMDD` and `YYYYMM` (also on a number column), `YYYY-MM`, `MM/YYYY`, `MM.YYYY` and `YYYY`; the compact ones take exactly 8, 6 or 4 digits (`2024115` is refused, never guessed), `YYYY` on numbers says "read as years", and `MM.YYYY` on a column the parser read as numbers (`03.2020` as 3.202) pads four decimals only when every cell then is a month of a year 1000 to 2200, at most 300 years apart, so a column of ordinary decimals (`2.2`, `3.5`) is refused. Since live test round 7 of 2026-10-16 the parser itself reads a column of months (`2025-01`), years 1000 to 2200, as dates, each the first day of its month as `set_type` `YYYY-MM` writes it, with a warning that names the reading (`parse._as_periods`): read as text, a monthly series had put `GWh` on x. A span that only looks like a month stays text, because a date would change what the cells mean: seasons (`2009-10`, `2010-11`, each second part the next year's last two digits) and bins (`2000-05`, `2005-10`), and any column whose name says ids, codes or seasons (`Saison`, `Code`, `Nr`). Quarters (`2025-Q1`) stay text too: as a date they would lose the spelling that says quarter; `set_type` reads them on request. The text labels of an `aggregate` by `month` or `quarter`, and the names a `pivot_longer` of month headers writes, stay text through the re-parse. A role that labels (`categorical`, `text`) takes a date column, so the months of a heatmap or a grouped bar chart keep their role; by default only a required role takes one, only up to 60 distinct dates, and after a real text category (`ts, host, latency` keeps `host` as the bars; 20,000 timestamps are never a stacked bar's components). Dates are read with Python's parser and stored at microseconds, so years outside pandas' nanosecond range (1677 to 2262) no longer crash the tool. A date column whose every day is at most 12 keeps its day and month order open from version to version (a warning carried by `with_date_notes`), and `set_type` swaps it in either direction: `MM/DD/YYYY` on a column read day first, `DD/MM/YYYY` or `DD.MM.YYYY` on one read month first. All this came from live test round 3 and its review: a MeteoSwiss table of `Year` and `Month` had no date for `line-timeseries-rolling`, the root tried `run_data_code`, which cannot read the user's table, and then asked the user to add the column by hand; the root prompt now names the operation and forbids handing data work back when an operation can do it. A date column the change made (a datetime column the version before did not have) fills an open required date role in `carry_bindings` (owner decision of 2026-10-15: code, not the model). No expression language (an injection surface), no joins, no model-guessed fills: the operations only move, type, aggregate, drop, label or compute from cells that are there, and `ToolSafety` validates the list against the same models before the tool runs. The server applies them with pandas to the stored canonical CSV, re-parses the result with `parse_dataset` (the dataset judge does not run again: the only new text is a rename's name or a `classify` label, which the model writes from a user message the scope judge already checked) and stores it as the next dataset version (`agents/anyplot/dataset_versions.py`): the previous version joins `anyplot.dataset_history`, which keeps the last five (older ones leave the store; the idle sweep takes a session's history only together with its current version), `anyplot.dataset_version` counts up from the paste's 1, the bindings are carried over (`carry_bindings`: the ones still valid stay, the roles left without one get the defaults), and the confirmation drops by itself because its fingerprint names the dataset id. The tool answers the change lines (one English line per operation with its counts, fenced as `<tool_notes>`) and the new profile (fenced as `<user_data>`), or a `PrepareError` code (`unknown_column`, `type_mismatch`, `empty_result`, `too_wide`, `invalid_operation`) with a message that names columns at most. The page learns about a changed dataset from the `done` event: after a turn that changed the dataset id it carries `dataset: {preview, profile, bindings, warnings, roles, changes, version, context_lines}`, the dataset route's shape plus the turn's change lines (`roles` since the formulas below), and `data_confirmed: false`; the BFF relays a `done` of up to 512 KiB whole (every other event stops at 64 KiB), which the largest dataset the parser accepts fits (`agents/stream.py`, `MAX_DONE_EVENT_CHARS`). `POST /v1/sessions/{sid}/dataset/undo` (the user's `.undo()` button; the model has no undo) restores the newest history entry as the current dataset, bindings carried over, unconfirmed again, and answers like the dataset route (`422 nothing_to_undo` for a paste that was never changed). The step-1 summary is the action `{"action": "summarize_data"}` on the messages route (judge skipped, like `create_plot`; since 2026-10-14 the turn also carries the notes pasted with the table, already judged at the paste, after a preamble and fenced as `<user_notes>`, and `get_dataset_profile` returns them under `user_notes` for later turns, with a fixed session-block line saying they exist, so the root uses them as context for the whole chat and acts on a clear request in them at once, the plot itself still waiting for the user's confirmation): the root reads the profile, which now carries `mean` and `median` for numeric columns, and answers in two to four sentences (rows, columns with types and units, missing values, values that look like percentages, the proposed bindings, and since 2026-11-01 a question only when the data would be read wrong without the answer, such as dates whose day and month order reads both ways), never claiming the data is right: the user confirms. The live eval `agents/evals/prep.py` scores the root's choices on `agents/evals/fixtures/prep/` by exact operation match, order-insensitive, with an 80 % gate (see [Run the prep eval](../../agents/README.md#run-the-prep-eval)). The page asks for the summary right after every parse that stores a dataset (a paste, or a transcription's `.use()`; never after an undo or a library switch), so it is the first turn in the thread, and the composer takes data requests in step 1. A `done` with `dataset` replaces the preview, the profile and the bindings, takes the page back to step 1, and shows a `# changed` block above the preview with the change lines and `.undo()`; after an undo the block names the restored version and keeps `.undo()` while the version is above 1, until the service answers `nothing_to_undo`.

Built since 2026-10-15 (questions): a question never changes the data. In the live test of 2026-10-15 the root answered "which country had the highest per-capita emissions in 2023?" correctly, but by filtering the user's dataset to the rows of 2023 with `prepare_data`, which stores a version, and then asked the user to `.undo()` it. The root tool `query_data(operations, columns, limit)` (`agents/anyplot/data/query.py`) applies zero to five operations of the same closed set as `prepare_data` (no free expression) to a copy of the current version and returns the first `limit` rows (1 to 25, default 10) in at most 8 columns, fenced as `<user_data>`, with the result's row count, a `truncated` flag and the change lines; the result is cut by rows to its 16 KB cap. Nothing is stored: the dataset, its version, the bindings and the confirmation stay as they are. The root prompt says that `prepare_data` is only for a change the user asked for, and that a question (a maximum in a year, a share per year, a trend) goes to `query_data`; a computed column the user then wants in the data is a `prepare_data` with the same operations.

Built since 2026-10-11 (formulas): owner decision of 2026-10-11, a function such as "sin x von -pi bis pi" is a deterministic data source in step 1, not a path around the data layer. The root tool `generate_data(variables, series)` takes a spec, never rows: one or two variables, each a range `{name, start, stop, points}` (`start` and `stop` are expressions over numbers and the constants `pi`, `e` and `tau`; 200 points by default, at most 5,000; two variables make a grid of every pair, the first variable slowest, at most 10,000 rows), and one to ten series `{name, expression}`. The server computes the table with NumPy (`agents/anyplot/data/generate.py`). The expressions are a whitelist over Python's `ast`, evaluated by walking the checked tree and never passed to `eval`: numbers, the variables, the three constants, unary `+` and `-`, `+ - * / ** % //`, parentheses, and the functions `sin`, `cos`, `tan`, `asin`, `acos`, `atan`, `sinh`, `cosh`, `tanh`, `exp`, `log`, `log10`, `log2`, `sqrt`, `abs`, `floor`, `ceil`, `round` and `sign` with one argument and `atan2`, `min` and `max` with two, at most 200 characters and 100 nodes per expression. Anything else (attributes, indexing, strings, comparisons, conditionals, lambdas, comprehensions, keyword arguments, `^`, `2x`, a `#` comment) is `invalid_expression` with a message that names the construct, such as "'^' is not a power; write x**2"; the typographic operators `−`, `×` and `·` read as `-` and `*`, so the change line quotes nothing but the checked expression. Every value is a float64 array under `np.errstate(all="ignore")`, so an overflow, a division by zero or a logarithm of a negative number becomes a missing cell, never an exception; a change line counts such cells per series (`y: 12 of 200 values are undefined and left empty`), and a series with no defined value is `undefined`. The other codes are `invalid_range` (an endpoint that is not finite, equal endpoints, or a range so narrow for its points that two of them round to the same value), `too_many_points` (a grid over 10,000 rows) and `too_large` (a table over the 2 MB cap of computed data, `MAX_COMPUTED_BYTES`, which no grid within the point limits reaches with the generator's cells since the cap rose from 200 KB on 2026-10-14; the message names about how many rows fit, and for a grid how many points each). Every value is rounded to eight significant digits and written as the parser writes a number, the variables before the series are computed, so every row holds `y = f(x)` for the `x` it shows; the table re-parses as number columns and becomes an ordinary dataset version: the next version through `push_version` when the session has data (undo restores the previous one), or version 1 the way the dataset route stores a paste when it has none; either way the confirmation drops by itself and the bindings are the defaults for the new columns, never the ones made for the old data, even where a column name such as `x` returns (`push_version(rebind=True)`). The tool answers like `prepare_data` (the change line, such as `generated 200 rows: x from -3.1416 to 3.1416, y = sin(x)`, fenced as `<tool_notes>`, and the new profile fenced as `<user_data>`), and `ToolSafety` validates the spec against the same model first, with the link rule over every string. The plot and its export (`plot.py` with `data.csv`) use the generated table like any other. Since a first dataset can now come from a chat turn, `done.dataset` carries the spec's `roles` too, at most 50, like the dataset route. The dataset route refuses pasted text that looks like a formula with `422 formula` before the transcriber would read it, because a model would compute the rows (`looks_like_formula`); only text within the chat's 2,000-character cap is refused this way, because the page's offer sends the refused text to the chat as a turn, and longer text reaches the transcriber, whose rubric computes nothing. The guard reads no delimited table, and reads the rest line by line and sentence by sentence, each of at most 300 characters: a formula is a call whose argument uses a variable (`sin(x)`, `sin x`, `√x`; never `Max(3)`, a name with a count), an assignment of an expression to a short name (`y = x**2`, `f(x) = 2x + 1`; never `Äpfel = 3x`), or a range phrase with numbers or `pi` next to a constant, a one-letter variable before it (`für n von 0 bis 10`), a power or a curve's name such as "Parabel" or "Sinuskurve". A unit is no sign of a formula (`m²`, `CO2 in t`), and neither is a word that data has as well, such as "curve", "Funktion", "exp" or "Quadratmeter". Text that holds its own values is data, whatever else it says: three numbers or more outside the formula's ranges, expressions, parameters and point counts (`Umsatz von 2020 bis 2024: 10 12 15`) send it to the transcriber. A formula request in one sentence of more than 300 characters is not caught; the transcriber's rubric covers it (compute nothing, write only the values the text holds). The scope judge treats a request to draw a function, curve or distribution shape as in scope, never `needs_data`, and four formula cases joined the prep eval.

Built since 2026-10-14 (data code): owner decision of 2026-10-14, a user may paste or type a snippet that generates the data (Python, JavaScript, R, a formula from a textbook, anything), and it must work offline. The root translates any non-Python input into Python and calls `run_data_code(code, source_language)`; `generate_data` stays until the evals show the new tool covers it. The server checks and runs the code in these steps (`agents/anyplot/tools/session.py`):

1. `code/data_code.py` strips a top-level `if __name__ == "__main__":` guard (its body dedented), and the validator's DATA profile (`validate_data_code`) checks the result: every SECURITY rule except the save target (a snippet saves nothing, so since 2026-10-25 every save name and `.open` is a `banned-call` in it); the import surface COMMON plus the standard `random` and the scipy computing submodules (no plotting library, no sklearn, no statsmodels, whose `datasets` reach files and the network); every private attribute banned on any receiver; a top-level assignment to `df`; and a `literal-data` rule that counts numbers and text values (list, tuple and set elements and dict values, never dict keys) in every outermost literal container, a repetition by an int literal counted as its copies, at most 20 of either per container and 40 in the file, so the model cannot write rows from memory. A file, URL or network finding carries the fixed message "paste the file's contents in the data panel instead". The findings reach the root fenced as `<tool_notes>`; the root fixes the code and calls once more at most (ToolSafety allows two calls per invocation).
2. The rate and the budget: a per-user daily count (`AGENT_DAILY_DATA_RUNS`, 30), checked and booked before the run, and the run queue's slot with the turn's start in the rate window (`RequestLedger.run_slot`, the same helper the pipeline awaits), taken mid-turn while the stream reports the queue position; a full queue or an expired wait ends the turn with `error{code:"capacity"}`, and a run past the user's `AGENT_USER_MAX_RUNS` across sessions with `error{code:"run_limit"}`.
3. The run: `services.backend.run_data` in one slot of the render semaphore's run lane, through the renderer's `POST /run-data` (see [Render](#render)) with a time limit of `AGENT_DATA_RUN_TIMEOUT_S` (20 s, at most 30). The harness runs the snippet in data mode under an audit hook and writes `df` as `out.csv`, and its outcome as `status.json` (its exit code and, for a broken row, column or size cap, the measured value and the cap). The host takes the exit code from `status.json`, because Cloud Run's sandbox launcher reports every non-zero exit of the sandboxed process as its own death by SIGPIPE (exit code -13, with no `[end]` line in `sandbox.log`; seen on every failed render and data run from 2026-10-11 to 2026-10-14), which turned a too-large table into "an error the sandbox does not name" in the live test of 2026-10-14. The tool trusts only what it parses itself: the exit code, which maps to a fixed line, the cap numbers, which make the line actionable (`df's CSV is 4,493 KB, over the 2,048 KB cap: keep every 3rd row (df = df.iloc[::3]) ... and round the floats to the decimal places that still keep the values distinct`), and `out.csv` within the parser's caps. The harness never rounds or thins a table itself: no fixed rule fits every table (a fixed number of significant digits collapses large values that differ only in their decimals, such as 1,000,000.1 and 1,000,000.9), so the root chooses the decimal places for the data it computed (`df.round(3)` for values between -50 and 50), and the tool description and the root prompt name the 2 MB cap of computed data (raised from the 200 KB paste cap on 2026-10-14, owner decision, so the live test's 10,000-step Lorenz run, 637,727 bytes, and 10,001 rows of six full-precision columns, 1,138,766 bytes, fit as they are) and that rounding up front. Nothing the snippet printed reaches the model: an exception is named by its class and the line of `snippet.py` it was raised on (`gates.error_summary`), never by its message, which a snippet could fill with an injection.
4. The table: `parse_dataset(find_table=False)`, at least one number or date column, then the same pre-filter and dataset judge as a paste (`dataset_guard.check_dataset`, one function the dataset route calls too, so strikes, token booking and attribution stay in one place; a refusal is the tool's `data_refused`), then the store: the next version with the default bindings (`push_version(rebind=True)`), or version 1 without data. The version keeps the Python that ran (`StoredDataset.code`), which the dataset answers and `done.dataset` carry as `code` and the data panel shows collapsed under `# code that made this data`. The change line is `computed from your code: <rows> rows, <columns> columns`.

The dataset route classifies a paste before it parses it (`data/classify.py`): code answers `422 code` (at any length, because code is never a table; `np.linspace(0, 10, 100)` would otherwise parse as a one-row table), a formula within the chat's cap `422 formula`; the page offers `.send()` for either when the text fits a chat message, and asks to shorten longer code. The scope judge calls a snippet or formula whose job is to generate the plot's data `in_scope` in any language, code with another purpose (factoring a number, "run this script") `out_of_scope`, and code that touches files, the network or the system `attack`. `AGENT_DATA_CODE=false` switches the tool off (it answers `unavailable`); a renderer deployed before `POST /run-data` answers `404`, which the tool also answers as `unavailable`.

Built since 2026-10-28 (the admin fan-out, owner request of 2026-10-28): `.plot_all()` plots the confirmed table in every library of the page's header at once, for the admin's tests. Since 2026-10-30 (owner request) it is chosen in the header: an `all` pill after the library pills, a toggle, turns the composer's create action (`.create_plot()`, or `.start_over()` once the library has a plot) into `.plot_all()`; before that it was a row of its own under the composer. While `all` is pressed the page's own session stays the data session, and any library pill turns `all` off again, the page's own library without switching. In step 2 the message box waits, since the fan-out takes no typed text, and the line above it says to turn `all` off to chat; in step 1, with or without data, the chat about the data goes on as without `all`. Before the confirmation a click on `.plot_all()` shows the same notice as `.create_plot()`. While the panel is open, the library pills, `all` and `.plot_all()` are disabled, and its `.close()` returns the focus to `.plot_all()`. The page opens a session per library (`FanOut` in `app/src/sections/agent-chat/`), and starts `create_plot` in at most `AGENT_USER_MAX_RUNS` sessions at a time, which `GET /v1/status` reports as `user_max_runs` (owner decision: a pool in the page, no server limit raised). Every run goes through the run queue like any other, and every session is deleted when the panel closes or the page goes.

Since 2026-11-01 (owner decision) the server copies the page session's confirmed current data version into each fan-out session (`POST /v1/sessions/{sid}/dataset/copy` with `{from_session}`, `copyData` in the page), so `.plot_all()` plots exactly what the user confirmed, whatever its origin: a paste, a file, a table the assistant changed (`prepare_data`, `.to_long()`) or computed (`generate_data`, `run_data_code`). The copy is the stored version as it is (`DatasetStore.copy`): the same canonical CSV, dtypes, column order, notes, file name, `source` and `code`, nothing parsed or judged again, so a library costs no paste and no model call, and one chat turn, its `create_plot`, against `AGENT_USER_MESSAGES_PER_MINUTE`. The bindings, their confirmation and the session language come along; the copy is the target's version 1 with no undo history, and the session state's `anyplot.dataset_copied_from` (the bundle's `session.dataset_copied_from`) records the source's version number. Both sessions are looked up under the caller's user id, so another user's session is `404 session_expired` and no data crosses users. The target must be a fan-out session (`source: fanout`) of the same spec without data (`409 has_dataset`), and the source must hold data (`422 no_dataset`) confirmed with its current bindings (`422 unconfirmed`); neither may have a turn, a render or a paste in flight (`409 run_active`). While the panel is open the page also locks the data card (`DataPanel`'s `locked`, with a line that says to close the panel first), so no paste, binding change, confirmation or undo can land between the copies of the libraries, which a new paste's version number (1 again) would not reveal to the server. Each copy takes the dataset's size in the store again, so a counter of its own bounds it (`COPIES_PER_MINUTE` 30, `COPIES_PER_DAY` 150 per user in `agents/anyplot/plugins/ledger.py`, constants like the outcome beacon's; a per-user byte budget in the store is the follow-up before public use, see [Risks](#risks-and-mitigations)). The `dataset_copy` attribution line carries the outcome, the row and column counts, the version number and the source session's salted hash, never data, and a copy writes no `data_input` telemetry line. Until 2026-11-01 the page posted the paste its own session stored again (`DatasetState.upload`, `replayData`) and kept `.plot_all()` disabled for changed or computed data; against a server without the copy route (`404 not_found`, the page deployed first) it still does that where the data came from a paste or a file, and otherwise each library stops with `copy_unavailable`. Since the same day the pill is in the URL as `&all=1`, which a reload presses again once the libraries are known, and a library switch writes the library's `language` to the URL along with `library`.

### Session language

Built since 2026-10-12 (session language): owner decision of 2026-10-12, a session has one language for the replies and for every text in the plot (since 2026-11-01 a plot keeps its own, see below), and it is server-set state (`anyplot.language`, `anyplot.language_source`), never a rule the root re-derives each turn. It starts as `en` with the source `default`, whatever the browser's locale, so a message such as "sin x" gets an English plot. A language is a short name or code that matches `^[A-Za-z][A-Za-z -]{0,39}$` (`agents/anyplot/language.py`); a known English name or endonym is stored as its code (`Deutsch` and `German` as `de`), anything else as typed (`Swedish`).

Since 2026-10-14 (owner request after a live session whose German notes and data got an English first summary), a paste sets it before any typed message, with the source `pasted` (`language.pasted_language`): the language of the notes kept around the table, as the scope judge names it when it screens them (`lang`), else the language of the column names, as the dataset judge names it for the part of its input that holds them; both judges already return `lang`, so this costs no extra model call. Both answer `en` for text without a clear language, so `en` is no signal and keeps what is there: headers such as `temperature_2m_max` or `x, y` leave the default, and an English paste after a German one leaves `de`. A paste writes only over `default` and an earlier `pasted`, never over `detected` or `chosen` (it reads the session again after the judges, so a choice made meanwhile stands), and the dataset route answers the session language as `language: {language, source}` so the page's chip follows at once; the thread shows one quiet line ("detected from your pasted data") and the chip `lang: de · from data`. A paste without notes leaves the English `[action]` line as the summary turn's only user text, and in the live test of 2026-10-14 the summary after a German CSV came back in English in 3 of 4 probe runs; so for a session language the server knows by name (`language.ENGLISH_NAMES`) the session block adds `- Reply language: German; ...` and an action message ends with `Reply in German, the session language.`, both from the server's table, never from the stored value (afterwards 8 of 8 probe runs in German with the session-block line alone, and 3 of 3 with both). A code check of the reply's language with one re-ask is not built. The session's first in-scope typed message sets it to the language the scope judge names for that message (`lang`, which the judge returns for every free-text turn), with the source `detected`, also over `pasted`. An `en` verdict counts only for a message of at least three words, because the judge also answers `en` for a message without a clear language: `sin x` keeps the default, and the next typed message is judged again. A later typed message never changes a recorded value; a refused message and the actions `create_plot` and `summarize_data`, which skip the judge, never set it; and the keys outlive a library switch. The chip in the chat header (`lang: en`, `lang: de · from data`, `lang: de · detected`, `lang: de · chosen`) lists the ten common languages, the site's ten most used visitor languages in Plausible in that order (`en`, `zh`, `de`, `vi`, `es`, `fr`, `nl`, `pt`, `ru`, `it`), and a free-text field for any other. A choice calls `PUT /v1/sessions/{sid}/language` and sets the source `chosen`, which detection never overrides. A request in the chat ("labels in English", "Beschriftung auf Deutsch") makes the same change through the root's `set_language(language)` tool, which the server validates like `set_bindings` and refuses while a plot run is active. Its result carries no value: it says whether the session language `changed` (`de` against `Deutsch` is no change), for the reply, and whether the plot is `plot_outdated`, that is, whether the library's latest rendered version has its text in another language than the plot language. Only `plot_outdated` makes the root write the next version in the new language, because the two can differ: a plot made by the `create_plot` action before any typed message stays English after a later German message records `de`.

Since 2026-11-01 (owner decision after a live test that day) the plot's text has its own language. In that test a German paste set `de`, the first plot came out German, and the English follow-up "Use a log scale on y" recorded `en` and made the adapter translate the title and switch to English number format, while the axes and months from the data stayed German; and "Make the title German", typed in English, made the root's `set_language` turn the replies German too, since `set_language` then set the one language for both. Now that request changes only the plot's language (`applies_to` `plot`) and the replies stay English. The plot language is resolved once per run (`session_state.plot_language`): the language the user asked for the plot's text (`anyplot.plot_language`, written by the chip and by `set_language`), else the language the session's latest rendered version is written in, in any library, else the session language. So the first plot follows the paste, the first typed message or the chip as before, and every later version keeps the language of the plot the user has: a follow-up message in another language changes the replies, never the plot. `set_language(language, applies_to)` takes `plot` ("labels in English", "make the title German"), `replies` ("answer in French") or `both` (the default, which the chip also does). `replies` stores the plot language as it resolves at that moment, so a first plot made afterwards does not follow the new reply language. The fan-out's dataset copy carries the page's plot language too. The paste still counts toward the session language as described above; the live test's first plot was German for that reason.

Every prompt that carries a language fences it, data, never an instruction, because a chosen language is free text; the value is still the language to write in. The session block names how the session language was set on a fixed line (`LANGUAGE_SOURCE_LINES`: "chosen by the user (in the chat header, or asked of you)") and fences it as `<reply_language>` under a heading that says to write the replies in it; since 2026-11-01 a plot language that differs follows as `<plot_language>`, under a heading that says it changes only through `set_language`. The adapter and the reviewer get the plot language as `<plot_language>`. Since 2026-10-15 the block stands in the root's system instruction (see the agent table above), and the root prompt says it is part of the instruction, never of the user's message, to follow it and never to question it; until then ADK sent it inside the user's turn, between its instruction markers, and a production session of 2026-10-12 (`20261012T211551Z-bcc5bcf1`, "klingon" in the chip) had the root refuse it as an injection and stay in English, and on 2026-10-13 the page's English `[action]` messages still set the reply language until the prompt said they do not. A language the model cannot write gives English with one sentence that says so, and the adapter notes it in `changes`. The adapter's `AdaptRequest` and the reviewer's `ReviewRequest` carry the plot language as `plot_language`. The adapter writes the title, axis titles, legend entries and annotations in it, while column names and labels from the data keep their words and language (since 2026-10-16 an identifier is written as text for a reader, `Reaktionszeit_ms` as "Reaktionszeit (ms)", per the style guide's "Axis and Legend Titles"); a change whose base version was written in another language carries `language_changed`, and its literal budget of 300 characters grows by 1.5 times the literal text the plan replaces, up to the first adaptation's 2,000, so a translation fits but a long new annotation on top of it does not. The reviewer judges the plot's own text against it under VQ-06, fail-open: a title or an annotation plainly in another language fails, a name or a label from the data never does (a raw identifier as an axis or legend title does, since 2026-10-16), and a text whose language it cannot judge is no defect. Code, comments, the `changes` notes and the root's `change_request` stay English. After every run, `done` carries `language: {language, source}`, so the page syncs its chip after a turn in which detection or `set_language` changed it. The fixed refusals keep their hand-written lines, in every chip language and Polish, with the English fallback: a judged refusal uses the message's language, and a refusal sent before the judge (`budget`, `unsupported_content`) uses the session language's code (`language.refusal_lang`: a code as it is, a known name such as `Deutsch` through a small table, anything else English). Versions made before a change keep their text, each version stores the language it was written in, and the feedback bundle carries `session.plot_language` and `plot_language_source`. Fonts for non-Latin scripts are the renderer's concern (a separate change). One register per language (live test round 4 of 2026-10-15, a German summary said "Aus Ihren Notizen" where the rest of the chat said "du"): the fixed refusals address the user informally where the language has the choice (du, tu, tú, je), `root.md` tells the root to keep that register in every reply, and `tests/unit/agents/test_reply_register.py` checks the fixed texts for the formal words of German, French, Spanish and Dutch.

### Request flow for "Find the right plot type" (phase 2)

The compact `id|title|cat:tags` index (about 13.7k tokens, cache-eligible) is appended to the root's `static_instruction` in phase 2 only; step 1 is page-scoped and the index would cost those tokens on every root call. The root calls `find_specs` (AND across categories, OR within one, values checked against the tag vocabulary) and `get_spec_knowledge` (including the synced "What a good version looks like" bullets) for at most three candidates, derives filters from the pasted profile, and replies with `[[spec:id]]` tokens that the UI turns into cards. `select_spec` validates the choice and swaps the snapshot. Embeddings (pgvector 0.8.5 on Cloud SQL PostgreSQL 18 plus gemini-embedding on `eu`) are added only if evals show that the index is insufficient.

### anyplot-agents internal API (`/v1`)

Callable only with Cloud Run IAM; the BFF mirrors it under `/debug/agent`.

| Route | Body and result | Errors |
|---|---|---|
| `GET /v1/status` | `{libraries, model, location, version, provider, waiting, in_flight, user_max_runs}`: `waiting` entries in the run queue, runs `in_flight`, and `AGENT_USER_MAX_RUNS` | |
| `POST /v1/sessions` | `{user, spec_id, library, locale, snapshot: CatalogueSnapshot{spec_id, title, description, data_roles[], notes[], code, library_version}, source?}` (at most 64 KB; `source`: `fanout` only, for the admin fan-out's sessions, whose usage telemetry it files apart from `live`) → `{session_id, eligibility}` | `422 not_eligible` |
| `POST /v1/sessions/{sid}/library` | `{library, snapshot}`; keeps dataset and bindings | `422`, `409 run_active` |
| `POST /v1/sessions/{sid}/dataset` | `{text, confirmed?, source?}` → `{preview, profile, bindings, warnings, context_lines, roles, version, language}`; `source` is the name of a dropped file (at most 1,024 characters), screened on its own and answered as `file_name`, never a note; `context_lines` are the judged notes pasted around the table; `language` is the session language as `{language, source}` after the paste, which sets it (`pasted`) when nothing typed or chosen has (see [Session language](#session-language)); `roles` lists the spec's data roles as `{name, kinds, required, variadic, description, non_negative}`; a paste is `version` 1 and drops an earlier paste's undo history | `413 too_long`, `422 unparseable`, `422 formula` (text that asks for a formula, which the root's `generate_data` computes in the chat; never transcribed), `403 data_refused`, `422 code` (a program that generates data, which the root's `run_data_code` runs in the chat; checked before any parse) |
| `PUT /v1/sessions/{sid}/bindings` | `[Binding]`, validated by `bindings.apply` | `409 run_active`, `422 invalid` with the check's `errors` lines |
| `PUT /v1/sessions/{sid}/language` | `{language}`, a short name or code that matches `^[A-Za-z][A-Za-z -]{0,39}$` → `{language, source}`: the user's choice from the chip in its stored form (`English` comes back as `en`), source `chosen`, which detection never overrides; the next reply and the next version use it (see [Session language](#session-language)) | `409 run_active`, `422` (the pattern) |
| `POST /v1/sessions/{sid}/dataset/convert` | `{data_base64, sheet?}` → `{text, format, kind, rows, columns, sheet, sheets, sheet_count, warnings}`: a dropped binary data file converted in the renderer's sandbox (`POST /convert`, `harness.py --convert`) into the text the paste box takes; nothing stored, no model call | `413 too_long`, `422 invalid`, `422 file_<reason>` with `measured` and `limit` for a broken cap, `403 session_closed`, `409 run_active`, `429 rate_limited` / `daily_limit` (`AGENT_USER_CONVERSIONS_PER_MINUTE` / `_PER_DAY`) / `locked_out`, `503 capacity`, `503 convert_unavailable` |
| `POST /v1/sessions/{sid}/dataset/confirm` | → `{confirmed, rows, columns}`: step 1 of two, the user confirmed the dataset with its bindings | `409 run_active`, `422 no_dataset` |
| `POST /v1/sessions/{sid}/dataset/undo` | → `{preview, profile, bindings, warnings, context_lines, roles, version}`: the version before the root's last `prepare_data` or `generate_data` is the current dataset again, bindings carried over, unconfirmed | `409 run_active`, `422 nothing_to_undo` |
| `POST /v1/sessions/{sid}/dataset/copy` | `{from_session}` → the dataset route's shape (version 1) plus `confirmed`, `complete`, `missing_roles` and `copied_from`: the admin fan-out's byte-for-byte copy of the confirmed current version of `from_session`, a session of the same user and spec, into this fan-out session, with its bindings, their confirmation and the session language; no parse, no judge, no paste counted (see [the fan-out](#two-steps-data-first-then-the-plot)) | `404 session_expired` (either session, also another user's), `409 run_active`, `409 has_dataset`, `422 no_dataset`, `422 unconfirmed`, `422 invalid`, `429 rate_limited` / `daily_limit`, `503 capacity` |
| `POST /v1/sessions/{sid}/messages?theme=` | `{text}` (at most 2,000 chars) or `{action}` (`create_plot`, `summarize_data`), and the optional query parameter `theme` (`light` or `dark`, the site theme the page shows, which a plot run of the turn renders unless the user asked for a theme) → SSE `anyplot/1`, started at once (only a `plot_pipeline` or `run_data_code` call waits in the run queue, mid-turn); before the scope judge, `budget`, `session_closed`, `rate_limited` and `daily_limit` are fixed refusals right after `ready`; `done` carries `bindings`, `data_confirmed`, `language` (`{language, source}`) and, after a turn whose `prepare_data`, `generate_data` or `run_data_code` changed the data, `dataset: {preview, profile, bindings, warnings, context_lines, roles, changes, version}` plus `code` for a version `run_data_code` computed (`version` 1 when `generate_data` or `run_data_code` made the session's first data) | `413 too_long`, `409 run_active` (a turn, a toggle or a paste in flight in this session), `422` for a `theme` other than `light` or `dark`; a full queue is the stream's `error{code:"capacity"}` since 2026-10-14 (`503 capacity` before the stream until then), a sandbox run past the user's `AGENT_USER_MAX_RUNS` the stream's `error{code:"run_limit"}` since 2026-10-21 |
| `POST /v1/sessions/{sid}/cancel` | Sets `abort_signal` and cancels the task; a waiting sandbox run leaves the queue | |
| `POST /v1/sessions/{sid}/versions/{version}/render` | `{theme}` → `{status: ok\|needs_attention\|failed, reason?, artifacts}`: the theme toggle renders `theme` of a finished version (0 is the latest) from its stored run form, synchronously, with no model call; `reason` is `canvas_padded`, `render` or `error` | `404 not_found`, `409 run_active` (the session has a turn, a toggle or a paste in flight; per session since 2026-10-21), `503 capacity` (no render slot in time, or the render store is full) |
| `GET /v1/sessions/{sid}/artifacts/{name}?v=&theme=` | Allowlist `plot-light.png`, `plot-dark.png`, the version's code artifact (`plot.py` for a Python library; `plot.R`, `plot.jl`, `plot.js` or `plot.tsx` for another language, since 2026-10-24), for a JavaScript version `plot.html` (since 2026-10-31), and `data.csv`; a PNG exists only for a rendered theme and carries the footer strip (a 3200x1800 render is served as 3200x1864) unless `AGENT_WATERMARK=false`; `plot.html` is the self-contained interactive page in `theme` (default: the version's), built on demand through the renderer's `POST /page`, never stored, wrapped in a sandboxed frame (`render/page.py`) and answered as an attachment with `Content-Security-Policy: sandbox` | `404`; for `plot.html` also `409 run_active` (it holds the session's registry entry like the theme toggle), `503 capacity`, `503 page_unavailable` (a renderer without `POST /page`, or none reachable), `500 page_failed` |
| `GET /v1/sessions/{sid}/bundle?version=&include_data=` | The server-assembled feedback case bundle; its images carry the footer strip like the artifact route; with `AGENT_KEEP_REFUSALS` it also lists the session's refused messages as `refusals` `[{verdict, lang, text, at}]` | `404` |
| `DELETE /v1/sessions/{sid}` | Purges the session, the dataset store and the render store | |
| any | `404 session_expired` after scale-to-zero or the idle sweeper | |

Headers: `X-Anyplot-User` (the HMAC id from the BFF) and `X-Request-Id`. Caller check: Cloud Run IAM verifies the ID token and forwards it with the signature replaced, so the app decodes the claims without signature verification and requires `aud` in `AGENT_SERVICE_URLS` and `email` in `AGENT_ALLOWED_CALLERS`. Cloud Run requires the service URL as `aud` even for a request to a tag URL, so `AGENT_SERVICE_URLS` holds the service URL, and a deploy smoke mints its token for that URL while it calls the `candidate` tag. When a request carries `X-Serverless-Authorization`, Cloud Run checks only that header and passes `Authorization` through unverified, so the app then reads `X-Serverless-Authorization` and ignores `Authorization`. A missing header is rejected when `ENVIRONMENT=production`; the check is skipped only in development. This is weak while anyplot-api runs as the shared compute service account; a dedicated API service account is an owner task before public use.

## Contracts

Schemas live in `agents/anyplot/schemas.py`: `ColumnProfile{name≤64, dtype, missing, unique, min, max, mean, median, top≤12, months?}` (`top` lists every value of a text or boolean column with at most 12 distinct ones, else its five most frequent, since live test round 8 of 2026-10-16, where the root never saw the sixth category `Übrige` of a six-row energy mix and told the user the plot had added it; `mean` and `median` for integer and number columns, six significant digits; `months` the months 1 to 12 of a text column whose every cell names a month, at least three, because the five top values cannot say whether `Jan` to `Mai` is the whole column, and `sort` orders such a column by the calendar, live test round 4 of 2026-10-15; the model-facing JSON leaves every empty field out), `DatasetProfile{rows, columns≤50, sample≤5 rows with cells≤40 chars fenced as <user_data>, source_format, decimal, warnings, list_columns?}` (`list_columns` the columns that came from the list fields of JSON records expanded into rows; a re-parse of a later version leaves it unset), `Binding{role ^[A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_]{0,31}$, column}` (digits and capitals because 21 of 325 specs name roles such as `log2_fold_change`, `lower_95` or `temperature_K`), `PipelineArgs{change_request≤600, base: catalogue|previous, theme: light|dark = light}`, `AdaptRequest{code (working form), profile, bindings, loader_columns, hints, change_request, feedback, previous_plan?, allow_full, plot_language, language_changed}`, `Edit{find (exactly one match), replace}`, `AdaptPlan{edits≤20, full_code≤24 KB (repair attempts only), title≤120, changes≤5}`, `ReviewRequest{render_id, code, bindings, profile_summary, spec_brief, change_request, gate_notes, plot_language}`, `Defect{id ∈ fixed set, theme light|dark|both|code, observed, target, likely_cause}`, `Verdict{ok, defects≤5}`, `PlotResult{status ok|needs_attention|failed|not_ready, reason?, attempts, artifacts, changes, residual_defects}`, where `artifacts` lists the PNG of the rendered theme only.

### Parse

`agents/anyplot/data/parse.py` has no ADK import. Limits: UTF-8 at most 1 MB for a paste (200 KB until 2026-10-14) and 2 MB for a table the service computed (`MAX_COMPUTED_BYTES`, passed as `max_bytes` by every re-parse of the service's own CSV), a canonical CSV of at most 4 MiB, 50 columns, 20,000 rows and 200 characters per cell. It rejects NUL and control characters, strips the BOM, applies NFC, and strips zero-width and bidi control characters. Headers are canonicalised (NFC, control characters stripped, names over 64 characters renamed, duplicates suffixed, empty names become `column_N`, each rename a warning), and the canonical names are written into `data.csv`, which is the single source of column names. Formats: JSON records, a column map, an object of values or an array of `[category, value]` pairs (the last two become the columns `category`, the keys kept as text, and `value`, source format `json_pairs`; nesting depth at most 2, except the nested objects of records described below; a repeated key keeps its last value, with a warning), a JSON-stat 2.0 dataset (since 2026-10-14, source format `json_stat`; see below), JSON Lines (since 2026-10-16, live test round 6, source format `json_lines`: when the whole text is no JSON but its first line is one JSON object, every non-empty line must be one and they read as JSON records; a line that is not names its number and the JSON error; up to 10 lines above the first object, a title or an export's comments, become notes when every line from there on starts with `{`; a dropped file named `.jsonl` or `.ndjson` reads as JSON Lines first, so one record is one row, while a pasted single object of values stays category/value pairs, with a warning when its values mix text and numbers), or delimited text with the delimiter sniffed from `, ; \t |`. Since 2026-10-16 every refusal names its cause (owner rule: people paste reasonably clean data, but a refusal says what it fails on): the agents service answers a refused paste with `cause`, the parser's message, which the BFF passes on one line of at most 300 characters and the page shows after its own text. A table whose rows have more values than its header names every ragged line with its field count, grouped by count, the first five lines of a group and the rest counted, and is never repaired; empty fields past the header's last column are left out with a counted warning, and the warning about short rows names their lines. Text that starts like JSON and is none of the readers' shapes names the JSON (or, behind an assignment or with a call in it, the Python) error's line and column, counted from the paste's own first line, with a hint for a comma after each line of objects, a missing comma and unquoted keys; JSON that Python reads as names (`true`, `null`, an unquoted key) gets the JSON error. The page shows the cause in place of its own line. A damaged file the converter refuses as `corrupt` names its broken part from a closed set (owner decision of 2026-10-16: `zip`, `workbook`, `sheet`, `parquet`, `arrow`, `npy_header`, `npy_data`), carried from the sandbox's `status.json` through the renderer's `ConvertRun.damage`, the agents service's `damage` and the BFF to the page, never as an exception's text; the renderer leaves the key out while it is empty, so an agents service deployed before the field reads every other answer. Since 2026-10-15 (live test round 3) two more shapes read. A table aligned with spaces (source format `whitespace`: a weather or statistics office's `.txt`, such as MeteoSwiss' homogenised monthly series) splits at runs of two or more spaces, so a header such as `Max Temp` stays one name; a tab is no separator there. Spaces are tried only when none of the four real delimiters finds a table (a CSV or TSV whose text fields hold spaces stays CSV or TSV), and they need stricter evidence, because they also separate the words of prose, the items of a list (`Hund 7`, which stays with the transcriber) and the keys and values of a metadata block: at least two data rows, a header line right above the first data row that holds no value (two or more years excepted, a wide table's `Land  2020  2021`), and no key-value block (a header whose first cell, or rows whose every first cell, ends with a colon). Owner principle of the review of 2026-10-15: a silent misread is the worst failure, so a refusal beats a guess. Single spaces split a table only of numbers and dates (every row at the header's width, no text cell, no two neighbouring fields that read as one value such as `1 234` or `2024-01-01 12:00`), with a warning on every such read: a single space also sits inside a two-word name (`Anna Lee`), so a table with a text column goes to the transcriber. A field in matching quotes loses them. A short row of a fixed-width layout (a blank cell) has its fields placed under the header's columns by their character position, never padded at the end, also below the last full row until a note, a source line or a summary row (`Total`, left out like other summary rows); a field that overlaps no column or more than one, columns whose own ranges overlap, and a row that does not line up, also a value row right below the table, are `unparseable`. The metadata block above such a table becomes notes like any other left-out lines. A line of the paste that only declares the missing-value mark (`Missing values: NA`, `nodata = -9999`; a sentinel number, negative or at least 999 in size, or one to three symbols or letters, never an answer such as `none`) counts as missing already while the table is found, so a row of marks stays a row; it blanks the cells of number and date columns only, a number matching by value (`-999.0`), with one warning that counts them, while a text category `M` or `NA` (Namibia) stays (owner decision of 2026-10-15). JSON records may nest objects, which become columns named with dots (`country.value`, `indicator.id`) down to three levels; an object nested deeper, or a field holding a list of objects or lists, is left out with one warning that names it (owner rule: canonical data is one flat CSV). A field holding a list of values expands to long format (owner decision of 2026-10-15, one measurement curve per record with `x: [...]` and `y: [...]`): the lists of one record expand together into one row per value, paired by position (so independent lists of one length, such as `tags` and `scores`, are paired too; the warning names every expanded field), the other fields repeated; an empty list leaves its cells empty, non-empty lists of different lengths in one record are `unparseable` with a message that names the record, the fields and their lengths, and so is a field that is a list in one record and a single value in another. When no text or whole-number field has a different value in every record, compared as `data.csv` will hold it (a unique measurement such as a temperature never counts), a first column `record_id` numbers the records, or `record_id_2` and so on when a field already has that name. Above the 20,000-row cap each record keeps evenly spaced values with its first and last one, the smallest thinning that fits, with a warning that gives the counts and the remedy (fewer points for one record, fewer records or points for several), which the root passes on. Records may also come wrapped as data APIs answer: an array of metadata objects and exactly one list of records (the World Bank's `[{page, pages, per_page, total, ...}, [records]]`), or an object with exactly one list of records beside other fields (`{"data": [...], "meta": {...}}`, also under `results`, `records`, `items` or any other key); the metadata's text, number and boolean fields go to the notes as `<name>: <value>`, and two lists of records, or metadata without records, are `unparseable`, since the parser takes no table by guess. A list beside the records counts as their schema only when its objects hold nothing but descriptor keys (`name`, `type`, `format`, `description` and the like) and their names cover every field of the records; descriptors beside an empty list of records are `unparseable`. A schema with rows of cells (CKAN, Socrata, Frictionless, pandas `orient="split"`: column names under `fields`, `columns`, `schema.fields` or `meta.view.columns`, rows as lists) reads as one record per row; rows without names that fit them are `unparseable`, so the schema is never taken for the data. BigQuery's own `rows: [{"f": [{"v": ...}]}]` shape is not read (its cells are a list of objects, which is left out, so the paste is refused). JSON-stat is still recognised first. The dataset pre-filter (`instruction_like`) reads the table with its constant columns, which come back into `data.csv` when the notes are dropped. The root's prompt lists every control of the chat page (`.parse()`, `.confirm()`, `.undo()`, the binding dropdowns, the language chip and so on) and forbids naming any other, since it once told a user to pick "a separator that recognises spaces", a setting that does not exist; `tests/unit/agents/test_prompt_controls.py` checks that each control the prompt names is a label in the page's code. JSON-stat is what statistics offices' APIs answer (the Swiss BFS PxWeb API, Eurostat, Statistik Austria, Statistics Norway): an object with `"class": "dataset"`, `id`, `size`, `dimension` and `value`; a JSON-stat 1.x bundle of one dataset is read too, a bundle of several, a collection or a lone dimension is `unparseable`. It is the one shape nested deeper than 2 (about five levels), and the 50-level bracket count still applies. The parser checks it strictly before it builds a row (as many sizes as dimensions, the product of the sizes at most 20,000 and equal to the number of values, `value` as an array or as a sparse object of positions, every dimension with as many categories as its size, `category.index` as an array or an object of positions), and refuses any mismatch as `unparseable` with a message that names no data. It flattens the dataset into a long table (owner rule: long format, no wide pivot; `prepare_data` can pivot later): one column per dimension with more than one category, named by the dimension's label (else its id), its cells the category labels (else the codes) kept as text, never typed and never missing (`NA` for Namibia stays `NA`, a year code stays text; a label two categories share gets each one's code in parentheses), then one column `value`, the only typed column, whatever the dataset's unit (a dimension named `value` in any case becomes `<label> (dimension)`, so the measure keeps the name), in the row-major order of `value`; a missing value stays empty, and `status` symbols are ignored with one warning. A dimension of one category is the same in every row, so it is no column: `<dimension>: <category>` goes into the notes (`context_lines`), after the dataset's `label`, its `source` and its `note` lines, so the pre-filter and the scope judge screen them like any pasted note; the warning only counts the dimensions left out. Since 2026-10-14 a pasted delimited or JSON table gets the same treatment: a text column with the same value in every row of at least two (`Kanton` = `Schweiz`, `Geschlecht` = `Geschlecht - Total`) moves into the notes as `<name>: <value>` with one warning that names the columns, never the values, unless fewer than two columns would remain; a re-parse of the service's own CSV (`parse_dataset(canonical=True)`: `prepare_data`, `generate_data`, `run_data_code`, `.to_long()`) keeps such a column, because an operation or the user's code made it. Only text columns move (owner decision of 2026-10-14). The values live in the notes only while the notes are kept: the parse keeps the table with its constant columns at hand (`ParsedDataset.with_constants`), the dataset judge reads that table, and a path that drops the notes (a refusal of the notes judge through `without_context`, or a store without screened notes such as a fixture seed or an eval's ingest) takes it, so the columns come back. Since 2026-10-15 a pasted column with a header whose every cell is missing (an API export's unused schema fields: an ECB CSV carried 14 such columns) leaves the table with one warning that names the columns, before the 50-column check, also when only one column with values remains; a canonical re-parse keeps it. A JSON table may therefore carry up to 200 keys (`MAX_RAW_COLUMNS`) before that check. A missing cell is the empty cell in `data.csv` and nowhere else: the run form's loader reads per column when the dataset has a text column (`keep_default_na=False` with `na_values` of `[""]` for a text column and the parser's missing tokens, bound once to `na_tokens`, for every other column, so the exported `plot.py` still reads a user's own R or spreadsheet export with `NA` or `-` in a number or date column), so a category `NA` (Namibia, North America) from a JSON key or a JSON-stat category survives the loader, `prepare_data` reads the CSV the same way, and a canonical re-parse keeps every text cell. A pasted `NA` is still missing (R and many exports write it so), except in a text column of two- or three-letter upper-case codes with at least two other codes, where it stays a code with a warning (owner decision of 2026-10-14). Pasted text is rarely a bare table, so since 2026-10-14 (owner rule) the delimited reader first finds the table among the lines (`table_block`): its data rows are the lines of the most common field count with numbers or dates in the same columns, the header is the line right above the first data row, and a title, an export's metadata (Open-Meteo writes two such lines above its header) or a sentence that explains the columns above it, a source line or a note below the last data row, and up to three summary rows at the end (`Total`, `Summe`, `Mittelwert`…) are left out, each kind with one warning in the preview; the delimiter is sniffed from the first 50 lines the same way, by the table with the most values. A table without a number or date column keeps every line, as before. The lines left out above and below the table are not thrown away (owner rule of 2026-10-14): they are the user's notes about the data, kept as `ParsedDataset.context_lines`. Each left-out record becomes one text line, its fields cleaned like cells (invisible characters removed, whitespace and line breaks collapsed to one space), the empty ones dropped and the rest joined with the delimiter's display form (`, ` for a comma, `; ` for a semicolon, ` | ` for a pipe, a space for a tab), so a sentence that its commas split reads as it was pasted. Summary rows are data and never notes. All lines together hold at most 2,000 characters, the chat message cap (`MAX_MESSAGE_CHARS`), with a warning when the cap cut them. The profile and `data.csv` stay as they were; a JSON or literal paste (except JSON-stat) and every re-parse of the service's own CSV (`find_table=False`) have no notes. A Python literal (`[`, `{` or `(`, also behind an assignment such as `cat_day = {...}`, which is stripped) is read like JSON with `ast.literal_eval`, which evaluates literals only; a name or a call is refused, and a bracket count that skips quoted strings and comments refuses more than 50 nesting levels before anything is evaluated. Text that starts with a bare `(` and is neither JSON nor a literal (a header such as `(in %) Land`, prose in parentheses) is delimited text as before. Everything is read as text first, then dates with explicit formats (an ambiguous day/month order is a warning), then numbers; a decimal comma is accepted only with a date guard. Since 2026-10-15 the parse keeps what the user pasted, or a warning says what changed (owner rule: precision is never lost silently). A number keeps its value, written in its shortest round-trip form (`1.50` is `1.5`, `1.5e3` is `1500.0`: the spelling changes, the value does not; owner decision of 2026-10-15: the value is enough, and one warning counts the numbers whose trailing zeros, exponent or plus sign went and names their columns); a number with more significant digits than a float64 holds (15 to 17, such as `0.1234567890123456789`, or a whole number past 2^53 beside decimals) is stored rounded with a warning that counts the cells, also from JSON and Python literals, whose float texts are kept until the typing reads them; a column of whole numbers past the int64 range stays text, so no digit is lost; an integer column's profile `min` and `max` are exact integers. In a text column, a cell whose surrounding spaces were removed (not the padding of a space-aligned table, nor the space after a CSV delimiter, which is layout) and a token such as `None` or `-` read as missing are counted in a warning; NFC composition gets one warning that counts the lines it changed. JSON's `NaN`, `Infinity` and `-Infinity` are missing values, counted in one warning. A whole number past int64 written with dot groups stays text too. Timestamps with fractions below a microsecond beside a date outside 1677 to 2262 stay text with a warning, because pandas holds a nanosecond only in that range and the loader would have left the column text without a word. The page's preview shows each cell with its whitespace: a line break as `↵`, a tab as `→` and every space of a run as `·`, drawn muted and named for a screen reader, so `a  b` never reads as `a b`; the profile's sample and top values, which the model reads, stay one line. Two roundings are deliberate and stay (owner decisions of 2026-10-15): the profile's `mean` and `median` carry six significant digits, for the model only (the page never shows them); and an integer past 2^53 in the profile loses its last digits in the browser, whose numbers are float64, which only touches the binding hint that names a negative minimum. Since 2026-10-15 numbers with unambiguous thousands groups are numbers too (`_as_grouped_numbers`): two or more dot or comma groups (`7.184.003`, `1,234,567`, which a German-format paste had left as text), a comma group before a dot decimal (`1,234.5`), or apostrophe and space groups (Swiss `1'234'567`, `1 234,5` with a no-break, narrow no-break or thin space too) where some cell holds two groups or a decimal part, the decimal mark the other one and the same in every cell; dotted dates stay dates. A single space or apostrophe group (`123 456`), IPv4 addresses and any column whose name says id, code, version, phone or postcode (`ID_WORDS`) stay as they are. A column of `1,234` shapes reads as comma thousands beside a comma-grouped column, and a column of `1.234` shapes as dot thousands beside a decimal-comma or a dot-grouped column, each with a note; a dot number elsewhere (a time `12.30`, a rating `4.5`) is no evidence, so a bare `1,200` stays a decimal comma with its note. In a file with an unambiguous decimal-comma column (`6,07`) the note goes. Since 2026-10-22 (issue #12224) a column of `1,234` shapes whose unit is a currency (`€ $ £ CHF EUR USD GBP`) reads as comma thousands with a note, since a money amount mostly has at most two decimals, so `$1,200` is 1200 while `$1,25` stays 1.25; a file that speaks for a decimal comma keeps the question instead (owner decision of 2026-10-22): one delimited by semicolons, or one with a column of dot groups (`7.184.003`, also one kept as text past int64), of `1.234` shapes or of comma numbers with a zero-led group (`0,125`), since a fuel price of `1,789 €` has three decimals. A whole number past int64 in a column read as comma thousands stays text, as with dot groups. A column whose every cell carries the same unit (`65 %`, `15,6 h`, `€ 5`, `CHF 1.200`, also with a no-break space between number and unit) is read as numbers, the unit moving into the column name (`Anteil (%)`) through the canonical-header path, with a warning, unless the header already names the unit; a column where only some cells carry a unit, or two units, stays text, as does one whose "unit" is a month, a boolean or an ordinal suffix (`5 Jan`, `1st`). The output is a canonical `data.csv` (comma, dot decimal, ISO dates) in a session-scoped dataset store (in memory, tied to the session's lifetime, the idle sweeper and the DELETE route; never a `user:` artifact, which would outlive the session and be listable by the model), plus the profile and default bindings (role dtype match, then name similarity, each column once; a categorical, text or boolean role takes a column with one distinct value only when no column with two or more fits, so a constant `Kanton` never becomes the series; since 2026-10-15 such a role ranks a real text column of 2 to 60 distinct values first, then an integer column of years (named so, `Jahr`, `year`, then an unnamed run of every year of its range), then a text column with more values, an ordinal integer (every value of a short range, `Quartal` 1 to 4), other integers of few values, a text column of numbers and any other integer last, and a number role that takes no dates ranks year columns after every measure (name similarity decides first; a price of 1,000 to 2,200 is no year without a year name or a full yearly run), while a time axis (`x (datetime/numeric)`) ranks them first among the numbers, so `bar-basic` on a year and two measures binds the year as the category and a measure as the value; since live test round 3 of 2026-10-15 a number role that takes no dates (a measure: `y`, `value`, `size`) also ranks weak measures last, `weak_measures`: a column with one distinct value in a table of at least five rows (a World Bank `decimal` of 0s), a year column, and a month, quarter or week of the year (an integer column named `Month`, `Monat`, `Quartal`, `Woche` and the like within that part's range, or every value from 1 to 12 or 1 to 4 beside a year column; unit and duration words such as `Minuten`, `Days to ship` or `kW` are measures); an optional measure role and every member of a number family past the first never take a weak measure, so they stay empty rather than plot a flat line or a month (owner decision of 2026-10-15: an optional measure takes any measure that is not weak), and a weak measure a family leaves out never becomes a series label;
